● I have enough understanding. Let me note one important architectural detail
  before writing: there's an environment-variable discrepancy worth documenting.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The Gemini key has three names but only one works. vite.config.ts defines
  process.env.API_KEY/process.env.GEMINI_API_KEY (sourced from GEMINI_API_KEY),
  the README says API_KEY, but ChatBot.tsx actually reads
  import.meta.env.VITE_GEMINI_API_KEY. Vite only auto-exposes VITE_-prefixed
  vars to client code, so VITE_GEMINI_API_KEY is the one that functions — the
  others are dead config. This is exactly the kind of cross-file gotcha
  CLAUDE.md should capture.
  - Styling is runtime-CDN Tailwind, not a build step. index.html loads
  cdn.tailwindcss.com and defines the theme inline — there's no
  tailwind.config.js, no PostCSS. Custom colors (accent: #CCFF00) and fonts live
  only in that <script> block.
  - ScrollTape drives the section rhythm. It owns the dark↔light contrast
  transitions between every section via scroll-linked useTransform color
  interpolation, decided by fromBg/toBg props.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● I've created CLAUDE.md. Here's what it captures that future instances couldn't
  easily discover on their own:

  The big-picture architecture:
  - It's Vite, not Next.js — the session metadata and next-themes dependency are
  misleading. next-themes is used purely for the dark class toggle.
  - Styling is runtime CDN Tailwind configured inline in index.html — there's no
  config file to find, so the design tokens (accent: #CCFF00, the four font
  families) only exist in that <script> block.
  - ScrollTape is the contrast engine — it sits between every section in App.tsx
  and drives the page-wide dark↔light transitions via fromBg/toBg props. This
  is the key to the recent commits about "alternating contrast" and is
  non-obvious without reading the component.
  - Content is fully hardcoded — profile in constants/profile.ts, section data
  inside each component, chat context as string constants in ChatBot.tsx.

  The gotchas that would cost a future instance real time:
  - The Gemini key naming conflict — three names referenced, only
  VITE_GEMINI_API_KEY actually works. The README and vite.config.ts are both
  wrong/dead.
  - No tests, no lint, no typecheck in the build — so don't suggest npm test or
  assume the build catches type errors.
  - Inconsistent asset filenames in public/.

  I followed the /init guidance: no invented "Common Tasks" sections, no generic
  advice, and I flagged the env-var discrepancy rather than parroting the
  outdated README.

  One thing worth your attention: the README still tells contributors to use
  API_KEY, which won't work. Want me to fix the README to match the actual
  VITE_GEMINI_API_KEY, or align ChatBot.tsx/vite.config.ts so all three names
  converge?

● Thought about it honestly. Here's my read.

  The blocker is technical, not just aesthetic: that robot is a hosted Spline 
  scene (prod.spline.design/UTIsnsf41Ax0oLMK). The glossy black material, the
  lighting, and the cursor-follow rig are all baked into that file on Spline's
  servers. I can't reskin it in code — making it light/matte means re-editing
  and re-publishing it in the Spline editor (your account), not a code change.

  The aesthetic verdict: for Swiss/Editorial light, the robot as-is is the
  single most off-brand element you could keep. That direction wins by restraint
  — off-white space, confident type, a strict grid, one accent. A glossy dark
  sci-fi humanoid is a maximalist "wow object" that pulls all attention to
  itself and fights the whitespace. It's also generic (a stock robot, not you).
  So keeping it as-is: no.

  But the interaction — something that quietly tracks the cursor — is genuinely
  nice and worth keeping in a calmer form. So this isn't binary "keep vs kill."
  Three honest paths:

  Next fork — what actually occupies the hero's visual slot. This is the biggest

● User answered Claude's questions:

● That's a confident, disciplined choice. Type-only Swiss hero is the hardest to
  do but the most rewarding — when there's no object to hide behind, the
  typography, spacing, and one interaction have to be perfect. Let me put the
  full hero design on the table.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Type-only heroes live or die on the typographic scale ratio and the grid.
  With nothing else competing, the jump from the giant name → role → body copy
  has to feel deliberate (a clear ratio like ~1.5–1.6×), and everything must
  hang off a visible margin/baseline grid. That discipline is the design.
  - You already load the right fonts. Space Grotesk, Manrope, and JetBrains Mono
  are in index.html. Reusing them = zero extra font requests, which matters for
  the light/fast feel. No need to add typefaces.
  - The cursor accent should be the only moving thing. In Swiss minimal,
  restraint reads as expensive. One reactive element against stillness beats
  lots of motion.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Hero Design — proposal

  1. Color palette (light, Swiss, one accent)

  ┌────────┬───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Token  │           Value           │                Use                 │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ bg     │ #FAFAF8 warm paper white  │ page background                    │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ text   │ #141414 near-black        │ name, headlines                    │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ muted  │ #6B6B6B                   │ secondary copy, labels             │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ line   │ #E4E2DC                   │ hairlines, grid, dividers          │
  ├────────┼───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ accent │ #2E5BFF ink blue          │ one CTA, the cursor dot, one       │
  │        │ (swappable)               │ underline                          │
  └────────┴───────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  The accent is the one personal knob. Ink blue reads "intelligent/engineer."
  Alternatives if you'd rather: a warm #C2613F terracotta, or a near-black
  "accent" (#141414) for the most austere look where color barely appears.

  2. Typography
  - Name (display): Space Grotesk, very large, tight tracking — clamp(3.5rem, 
  10vw, 9rem), line-height ~0.95.
  - Role + body: Manrope.
  - Kicker / micro-labels: JetBrains Mono, uppercase, wide tracking — this keeps
  a whisper of your technical identity without the cyberpunk noise.

  3. Layout (asymmetric editorial grid, generous margins)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DINESH KUMAR                          (nav handled       │  ← fixed top
  │                                         separately)       │
  │                                                           │
  │   ┌ // gen-ai & ml engineer        ·  ← cursor accent     │
  │   │                                                       │
  │   │  Dinesh                                               │
  │   │  Kumar.                            ← huge, left-anchored
  │   │                                                       │
  │   │  I build high-performance AI systems                  │
  │   │  and cognitive solutions.          ← one calm line    │
  │   │                                                       │
  │   │  [ View work → ]   Contact                            │  ← 1 solid
  accent btn + 1 text link
  │   └                                                       │
  │                                                           │
  │  Puducherry, IN          Available for work    ↓ scroll   │  ← thin baseline
  meta row
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
     strict left margin · lots of whitespace · hairline grid

  - Content left-anchored on a strong margin (not centered — centering kills
  editorial tension).
  - A faint baseline meta row at the bottom: location · availability · scroll
  cue. Replaces your dense 0x21_NODE clutter with two or three quiet facts.
  - Socials move out of the hero into the footer/nav (declutter), unless you
  want them kept.

  4. Motion & interaction
  - Entrance: name + role rise once with a soft fade (no blur storm). ~1 clean
  reveal, fast easing. Calm, not cinematic.
  - Cursor accent (the kept interaction): a small accent-colored dot near the
  kicker that eases toward the cursor with spring lag — subtle, present only in
  the hero. Ties into your existing custom Cursor.tsx.
  - Scroll fades the hero out gently (you already have this pattern).

❯ give the hero section proper entry animations , transistion , spacing , shape

● Approved and clear — building it now. I'll use my picks: Space Grotesk for the
  name and socials kept in-hero (restyled into a clean pill). Implementing the
  full Cool Lab hero with proper staggered entrance, cursor parallax, refined
  spacing, and consistent shapes.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The blue glow needs mix-blend-screen, not z-stacking. The robot's Scene
  paints its own near-black background, so a glow placed behind it would be
  hidden. Layering the glow over the scene with a screen blend makes it add 
  light to the dark pixels — it rims the chrome in blue instead of covering it.
  This is the trick that makes the robot feel lit rather than pasted on.
  - Orchestration via Framer variants + staggerChildren beats per-element delay
  props: one container drives the whole cascade, so retuning the rhythm later
  means changing one number, not ten.
  - prefers-reduced-motion is respected — parallax and the entrance cascade
  collapse to an instant, static render for users who ask for it. Cheap to add,
  and the right default.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● I now have full context — your character comes through clearly in the resume
  and docs: a solo builder who ships ambitious AI systems alone, values rigor +
  real-world usefulness over demos, curious, disciplined, top-achiever. The
  identity section should say that — not list tech.

  Here's my plan, then I build it (no more back-and-forth):

  Building:
  1. ScrollTextReveal — the word-by-word reading reveal (grey → solid on
  scroll), theme-aware.
  2. shapes.tsx — reusable clean motif (corner brackets + crosshair ticks) for
  the whole site.
  3. Rewrite Identity section (ProfileHeader) — manifesto centerpiece with the
  word-reveal, cyberpunk-framed portrait, principle chips, writer-voice copy.
  4. Fix ScrollTape — make section transitions theme-aware (no more stuck-dark).

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Color chemistry of neon lime (#CCFF00): it's a yellow-green that only "pops"
  against dark. On white it nearly vanishes (fails contrast as text). So the
  disciplined rule I'm applying site-wide: neon never carries body text — it
  appears only on dark backings (scanline over the photo, the dark transition
  band) or as small markers (dots, brackets, ticks). Black/white does the
  reading; neon does the signaling. That's "using neon properly."
  - The word-reveal uses opacity, not color — base ~0.15 of the current text
  color. So a single component reads as light-grey→black in light mode and
  dark-grey→white in dark mode automatically. No theme branching.
  - The transition bug is that ScrollTape hardcoded #050505; making its spacer
  bg-white dark:bg-background and the band bg-black dark:bg-accent ties it to
  the active theme.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Let me write the primitives first.

● Now let me verify the current Framer Motion pinned-scroll pattern via Context7
  (you asked me to use the connected MCP).

  Called plugin:context7:context7 (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both sources confirm the approach. The pin pattern is a tall outer container +
  a sticky top-0 h-screen inner, with useScroll on the outer driving the reveal
  — so the viewport "locks" while the words resolve, then releases.

  Here's the design plan I'm executing (monochrome, material depth, minimal
  motion):

  Palette (no neon, black/white shades)
    ink   #0B0B0C   snow  #F5F5F6   mist #8A8A8E
    pearl #ECECEE (light surface)   onyx #141416 (dark surface)
  Type   Syne (display, restrained) · Manrope (body) · JetBrains Mono (data)
  Signature  ACT 1: viewport-locked word-by-word manifesto reveal
             ACT 2: a tactile monochrome "identity plate" — grayscale portrait
  in a
                    claymorphic frame, a frosted-glass bio panel overlapping it,
                    a debossed monogram, grain texture for depth
  Motion  reveal + soft parallax + gentle hover lift. No 3D, no shimmer.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Material depth in monochrome relies entirely on shadow direction.
  Claymorphism = dual shadows (dark bottom-right + light top-left) so a surface
  reads as extruded; glassmorphism = backdrop-blur + a 1px top highlight so it
  reads as a pane floating above. With no color to lean on, the shadows are the
  design — so they're theme-specific (shadow-[...] dark:shadow-[...]).
  - Grain is what stops flat monochrome from looking cheap. A tiny inline SVG
  feTurbulence noise at ~5% opacity adds tactile texture without an asset
  request.
  - Pinning ≠ JS scroll-hijack. position: sticky does the lock natively (no
  scroll listeners fighting Lenis), and useScroll just reads progress — smooth
  and reduced-motion friendly.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Let me upgrade the reveal primitive to support external (pinned) progress:

  ⎿  Added 24 lines, removed 12 lines
       3

● This is great direction — and the "not locking" bug, the slideshow-y
  transition, and the reordering are all fixable. Let me diagnose the lock
  first.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Why the viewport wasn't locking: position: sticky silently dies if any
  ancestor up to the scroll root has overflow: hidden/auto/scroll. Your
  index.html sets overflow-x: hidden on html and body — that turns the body into
  a scroll container and breaks every sticky descendant. The fix is overflow-x:
  clip: it still prevents sideways scroll but does not create a scroll
  container, so sticky works again. One-line root cause.
  - Seamless black→white without color math: render the transition text in white
  with mix-blend-difference. It auto-inverts against whatever's behind it —
  white on black, black on white — staying perfectly legible through the entire
  morph. So I morph only the background color and the text contrast takes care
  of itself. No "slides."
  - Order = meaning: leading with face + plain-spoken bio, then the slow belief
  statement, reads like meeting a person before hearing their philosophy. Much
  better narrative than the reverse.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  html/body in index.html. This is what was killing every position: sticky. The

  releases — and the new transition pins too.

  more slideshow. The background morphs black → white smoothly while two calm

  and stays legible through the whole wash. It pins for breathing room; starts

● This is the most important section — first impression — so let me do it
  properly, not slap effects on. Two honest design realities up front, no
  sugarcoating:

  1. The hero has to stay dark, even in light mode. That robot is glossy black
  chrome on a hosted Spline scene — I can't recolor it, and on a white
  background it would look like a pasted-on cutout. A dark hero flowing into
  your white "About" via the black→white tape is the correct senior move, not a
  compromise. Monochrome works in our favor here: a black robot on a graphite
  stage is exactly the palette you want.
  2. "Match the section below" = match its language (Syne, grain, restraint,
  mono labels, no neon), not its literal background. I'll carry those through.

  My concept: robot dead-center as the subject, your name as a giant outlined 
  Syne backdrop it stands in front of, only "Gen AI / ML Engineer" as a label.
  Name + specialization, nothing else. Zoom-out entrance handed off cleanly from
  the preloader, very subtle light leaks, fully responsive.

● The preloader exits with an upward clip-wipe over the same #050505 as the hero
  — so if I keep the hero stage #050505, the reveal is seamless and the hero
  content can animate in right as the wipe finishes. Good handoff.

  Let me confirm the Spline load API so the zoom-out fires when the robot is

  Called plugin:context7:context7 (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed the API: onLoad gives me the moment the robot is actually ready, so
  the zoom-out won't fire on an empty canvas. Building now.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Honest take on "AI slop": the tell is usually decoration without hierarchy —
  five effects competing, a bright accent doing nothing, centered everything.
  The fix here is one subject (the robot), one type move (your name as a giant
  outlined Syne plate it stands inside), and everything else whisper-quiet.
  Restraint is what reads as "senior."
  - Zoom-out gated on onLoad: the robot starts at scale 1.25 / opacity 0 and
  settles to 1 only once Spline reports loaded — so you never see it pop in
  half-rendered. The name plate + stage animate in immediately on the preloader
  handoff, the robot arrives a beat later. That sequence is the experience.
  - Light leaks must be mix-blend-screen at ~8% — any heavier and it's a gamer
  wallpaper. On a near-black stage, screen-blended white just lifts the air
  slightly. Subtlety is the whole point.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  First, Scene gets an onLoaded hook and a clean monochrome fallback (the random
  stock forest image is gone):

● Typecheck clean. Here's what I built and the honest reasoning behind each
  call.

  Hero (Hero.tsx (components/Hero.tsx)) — rebuilt minimal/monochrome
  - One subject, centered: the robot now fills the screen centered (was
  right-aligned), standing inside a giant outlined "DINESH / KUMAR" in Syne —
  the font you like, used as the stage. The robot occludes the type → real
  depth.
  - Only name + specialization. Everything else is gone: no socials, no
  location, no "open for work", no engine toggle, no CTA button. Just a Gen AI /
  ML Engineer label up top and a scroll cue at the bottom. Per "we'll tell
  everything later."
  - Neon eliminated — pure white/grey on graphite. Nothing colored.
  - Zoom-out entrance gated on load: robot starts at scale 1.25 / opacity 0 and
  settles only when Spline fires onLoad, so it never appears half-rendered. Name
  + stage animate in first on the preloader handoff; robot arrives a beat
  later.
  - Preloader handoff is seamless — the preloader wipes upward over the same
  #050505, revealing the hero with no flash.
  - Subtle light leaks — two screen-blended white glows at ~7–10%, one slowly
  drifting. Barely there, just lifts the air.
  - Responsive — name scales on vw (22vw mobile → 16vw desktop), robot centered,
  label/scroll scale down. Reduced-motion fully respected.

  Transition (Marquee.tsx (components/Marquee.tsx) + scroll-tape.tsx 
  (components/ui/scroll-tape.tsx))
  - New copy that echoes your real ethos instead of buzzwords: "Generative AI · 
  Machine Learning" / "Built to hold up" / "From first idea to real use."
  - Softened the spring + widened the visible hold so it reads as a calm wash,
  less slideshow.
  - Hero stays #050505, so the tape's fromBg still matches it exactly — seamless
  black→white into About.

  Honest flags — two things I genuinely can't verify without you looking:
  1. Robot centering depends on the Spline scene's own camera. I centered the
  container, but if the robot sits off-center inside the hosted scene, it'll
  still look slightly off. If so, tell me which way and I'll nudge the Scene
  container — that's a 1-line fix, not a redesign.
  2. The giant name vs. robot balance — on some viewports the robot may cover
  too much of the name, or too little. Easy to tune the name size / robot scale
  once you see it.

● Good, clear feedback — and the screenshot helps a lot. Reading it like a
  senior designer:

  - The robot reads great but yes, full-body fills the frame and the legs add
  nothing — cropping to a bust (head → torso → hands) is stronger and more
  "portrait."
  - Your reference is the move: domain pills flanking the subject. From your
  resume, your real domains are Generative AI, Machine Learning, and AI Agents /
  multi-agent systems (StayBot, AURA) — that's what I'll put in pills, not
  buzzwords.
  - The giant outlined name fighting the robot was the weak part. I'll frame the
  robot with the name split to the sides instead of hiding behind it.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Cropping the legs without resizing: I keep the robot's scale exactly as-is
  and fade the lower ~30% into the stage black with a gradient overlay. That
  both hides the legs and grounds the figure ("emerging from shadow") — much
  classier than a hard cut, and it costs nothing.
  - Why pills beat a label: three glass pills around the subject create a frame
  (eye travels subject → role → subject), which is why the reference works.
  Frosted glass (white/6 + blur) instead of solid black keeps it
  monochrome-premium and lets the stage show through.
  - Name placement: split "DINESH" / "KUMAR" to the upper corners so it frames
  the robot instead of colliding with its head — the head sits dead-center, so
  anything centered there loses.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● Got it — current "Services" is really your skills registry, so it becomes
  Skills; the new Services = "what I can do for you", the next beat in the story
  (believe → provide → prove).

  Services I'd list (drawn from your resume + projects):

  1. Generative AI Applications — RAG systems and LLM apps with structured,
  reliable output (EcoBot, StayBot, LangLearn)
  2. AI Agents & Automation — multi-agent systems, tool-calling, MCP
  integrations that actually act (AURA, StayBot)
  3. LLM Fine-Tuning & Optimization — QLoRA fine-tuning, model routing,
  evaluation, cost-aware pipelines (EcoBot)
  4. Computer Vision & On-Device ML — YOLOv8 / OpenCV, OCR, real-time perception
  (AURA on-device)
  5. AI Backends & Deployment — FastAPI/Flask APIs, WebRTC, Docker, Cloud Run,
  observability (all projects)

  (That's 5 — honest and resume-backed. Could trim to 4 by merging 3 into 1.)

  Design idea (short): Not a card grid. A capability ledger — a large-type,
  monochrome numbered list continuing the Identity language (Syne, grain,
  glass). Each service is a full-width row; on hover/tap it opens to reveal the
  one-line description and the real projects that prove it. Eyebrow ties the
  narrative: "You know what I believe — here's what that produces." Minimal
  motion, depth from the open/close reveal, no neon.

  Want me to go with these 5 (or trim to 4), and is the "capability ledger /
  interactive list" direction good before I build?

● Honest answers, since you asked for honesty:

  - Technical Consulting — drop it. Your instinct is right. "Consulting" implies
  a track record clients pay for advice on. As a fresher it reads as
  overclaiming, and that one word can make the whole list look inflated. Cut it.
  - UI/UX Design — only if you actually design. If you deliver Figma files,
  wireframes, design systems → keep it. If you mostly build front-ends from
  designs, call it Front-end Development (or fold it into Web Development).
  Listing "UI/UX Design" with no design work behind it is the kind of padding
  that hurts a fresher's credibility. Your call — do you design, or build?
  - Overlap flag: "AI Agents & Automation" and "Automation & Integrations"
  collide. Either keep them separate cleanly (Agents = AI-driven; Automation &
  Integrations = scripts, APIs, webhooks, no-AI glue) or merge. I'd keep them
  separate so each is distinct.

  Proposed final list (6 — focused, honest, all resume-backed):

  1. Generative AI Applications — RAG, LLM apps, fine-tuning
  2. AI Agents — multi-agent systems, tool-calling, MCP
  3. Web Development — full-stack, React/Next.js + Flask/FastAPI
  4. Backend & API Development — FastAPI/Flask, real-time, deployment
  5. Mobile App Development — Android/Kotlin, Flutter (AURA on-device)
  6. Automation & Integrations — workflows, API/webhook glue, pipelines

  That's a strong, broad-but-believable 6. UI/UX is the only open question.

  One honest nudge: 6 is the upper limit before it looks like padding. For a
  fresher, a tight list you can clearly back beats a long one. If you want UI/UX
  in, I'd drop or merge one of the overlapping ones (e.g., fold Automation into
  AI Agents) to stay at 6.

  So: do you design (UI/UX in or out)? Lock that and I'll finalize the list,
  rename current Services → Skills, and build the new Services ledger.

❯ what will be teh best optimal next section                                    

● Honest answer: Skills, next. Two reasons, one practical, one strategic.

  Practical (this is the real one): right now the page is polished and coherent
  from Hero → About → Services… and then it falls off a cliff. The Skills
  section sitting directly under your new monochrome Services is still the old
  neon cyberpunk block. That hard clash is the single biggest thing breaking the
  "smooth, perfectly designed" feel — and it's the very next thing a visitor
  sees after the part you just nailed. Fixing the section adjacent to finished
  work matters more than jumping ahead.

  Strategic: it pairs tightly with what you just built — Services = what I can 
  build, Skills = what I build it with. Doing them back-to-back lets me make
  them feel like one designed system (shared type, grain, spacing, monochrome).

  One honest caveat as your designer: the current Skills block is heavy — 5 big
  panels with logo grids — and it partly repeats the proof you already show in
  Services. I'd slim it down: a tight, confident tech-stack section (grouped,
  monochrome, restrained) rather than the maximalist registry. Less is more
  here; it shouldn't compete with Services or Works for attention.

  After Skills → Works. That's the heavyweight — the actual proof recruiters
  care about most — and it deserves a focused pass of its own, not a rushed one
  bundled with Skills.

  So the optimal path: Skills (slim redesign) → Works (the big one) → 
  Achievements/Certs → Experience → Education → Contact.

  Want me to redesign Skills now — slimmed and monochrome to match Services?

● Yes — that's the Apple "muted body, bright emphasis" pattern, and it's a great
  call for adding hierarchy to body copy. I'll add it to About, Services, and
  Skills via a reusable Em / Mark so it's consistent. And you're right that
  Skills went too quiet — I'll give it life with a proper pause-on-hover marquee
  (edge-faded, alternating direction) so it moves but stays readable.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The Apple effect is a contrast trick, not a font trick: the body drops to
  ~grey-500 and only the emphasis stays full-strength + medium weight. If the
  body isn't muted enough, the highlight doesn't pop. So I lower the base color,
  not just bold the keywords.
  - A marquee only reads as "designed" with two things: an edge fade mask (tags
  dissolve at the row edges instead of hard-cutting) and pause-on-hover (so it's
  not a thing you fight to read). Without those it looks like a 2008 ticker.
  CSS animation-play-state: paused on hover beats JS here.
  - Reused one marquee keyframe driven by a --marquee-duration var, so each row
  can scroll at its own speed from the same animation.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● On the scroll-lock question — my honest senior answer: don't do it for 
  Services. Here's the reasoning, not sugarcoated:

  - Pinning + scroll-scrubbing a list means hijacking the user's scroll. For an
  informational section people want to scan, forcing them through a locked,
  one-by-one sequence is friction, not delight. It also fights trackpads, hurts
  accessibility, and gets janky on mobile.
  - We already spend one viewport-lock on the manifesto ("What I believe").
  That's the signature moment. Using the same trick again two sections later
  makes the whole page feel like a scroll-jack gimmick — the "tries too hard"
  tell. Restraint is the senior move: lock once, for the one thing that earns
  it.
  - The current click/hover accordion is better UX here: instant,
  user-controlled, scannable. Keep it.

  So: Services stays an accordion. I'll put the energy into the real problem you
  flagged — the layout breaking on your laptop.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Why it breaks on laptop: the hero positions name/pills with magic
  percentages (top-[19%], top-1/2, bottom-[16%]) tuned to your monitor's aspect
  ratio. On a shorter laptop those percentages land in different spots and
  collide. The fix is to stop positioning by % and use a full-height flex column
  with justify-between — it distributes name → pills → scroll across whatever
  height the screen is. Self-correcting on every display.
  - "Hiding behind the navbar": the navbar is position: fixed, so anchored
  sections scroll under it. The fix is a scroll offset equal to the navbar
  height (~100px) on nav clicks, plus top padding in the hero so content clears
  it.
  - Scrollbar: with Lenis driving smooth scroll, the native bar is redundant —
  hide it via scrollbar-width: none + ::-webkit-scrollbar.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Let me apply the fixes. First the hero — flex layout instead of magic
  percentages:

● Done. Typecheck clean. Here's everything in this pass:

  The scroll-lock call (your "should I?"): No — Services stays a click/hover
  accordion. Locking it would hijack scroll on an info section people want to
  scan, and we'd be spending the viewport-lock trick twice (manifesto +
  services), which cheapens it. Lock once, for the one moment that earns it.
  That's the senior call.

  Laptop placement — root-caused and fixed (Hero.tsx (components/Hero.tsx)):
  - Ripped out the magic-percentage positioning (top-[19%], top-1/2,
  bottom-[16%]) that was tuned to your monitor. The hero is now a full-height 
  flex column with justify-between: name row on top, side pills in the middle,
  third pill + scroll at the bottom — it distributes itself across any screen
  height, so it self-corrects on laptop vs. monitor. One layout, no duplicate
  desktop/mobile blocks.

  Navbar overlap fixed (Navbar.tsx (components/Navbar.tsx)):
  - Anchor scroll offset -20 → -100, so clicking any nav link lands the section
  below the fixed navbar instead of tucked under it. Hero also got pt-28/32 so
  its name clears the bar.

  Scrollbar removed (index.html): native bar hidden (scrollbar-width: none +
  ::-webkit-scrollbar), Lenis still scrolls smoothly.

  Sizing: trimmed Services/Skills vertical padding (md:py-36 → md:py-28) so they
  breathe on a laptop without feeling cavernous.

  One honest note on "each section must fit in exactly one viewport": I
  deliberately did not force every section to 100vh. Content length varies
  (Skills has more than Mobile App Dev), and clamping all of them to one screen
  would either cut content off on short laptops or leave huge gaps on tall
  monitors — that's a classic overconstraint. Instead I made them responsive and
  navbar-safe. If a specific section still feels oversized on your laptop, tell
  me which one and I'll tune that one precisely.

  Reload on the laptop and check: hero framing, nav-link landings (should clear
  the navbar), and the gone scrollbar.

● Typecheck clean. The Works section is rebuilt — here's the senior pass,
  honestly assessed.

  What it is now (Works.tsx (components/Works.tsx)):
  - A clean monochrome project grid (your reference). Media sits grayscale by 
  default and resolves to full color on hover, with a subtle lift, a zoom, and a
  "view ↗" chip — motion that informs (this is clickable/external), not
  decoration.
  - Minimal content per card exactly as you asked: title + category + year. No
  descriptions — those go on the per-project detail pages later.
  - Narrative tie continues: Skills (what I build with) → Works: "Selected work.
  Proof, not promises — a few systems I've built end to end." with a View all 
  work ↗ link.
  - Cards link out to each repo now; a final dashed "More on GitHub" tile keeps
  the grid balanced and becomes the gateway to the full archive page later.
  - Shares the site language: Syne, grain, mono labels, monochrome, no neon.

  Three honest flags — the real constraints, not sugarcoated:
  1. AURA and StayBot have no screenshots in your assets. I gave them
  typographic cover tiles (outlined name on graphite) so they look intentional
  rather than broken — but this section lives or dies on real visuals, and AURA 
  is your flagship. Getting even one clean screenshot/mockup each would elevate
  this section more than any code I can write. This is the #1 thing to fix.
  2. EcoBot's asset is a logo, not a UI shot (so it renders contained on a
  panel, not as a screenshot). A real interface capture would make it match
  GravitycARgo/LangLearn.
  3. Links: AURA, EcoBot, StayBot, LangLearn use your résumé-confirmed GitHub
  URLs. GravitycARgo's repo URL I guessed (/GravitycARgo) — please verify or
  send the right one. "View all" → your GitHub profile for now; it'll point to
  the dedicated Works page once we build it.

  Reload and hover the cards. Then the highest-leverage next move is screenshots
  for AURA + StayBot (and ideally EcoBot) — send them and I'll drop them in.
  After that, the logical next step is the per-project detail page (your
  reference's second screenshot) so "View all" and each card have somewhere rich
  to go.

● This is the big one — separate project pages, an "all work" page, and a
  featured-4 section with real effects. That needs client-side routing, which
  the app doesn't have yet. I'll add a lightweight hash router (no dependency,
  real shareable URLs like #/work/aura, back-button works) rather than pulling
  in react-router and restructuring everything.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Hash routing fits this app perfectly: it's a Vite SPA with Lenis smooth
  scroll on window. A hash router needs zero deps, survives refresh/deep-links,
  and doesn't fight Lenis — I just scroll-reset on route change. #/ = home,
  #/work = all, #/work/aura = detail.
  - One data source, three consumers: project content lives in
  constants/projects.ts. The featured grid (home), the all-work page, and each
  detail page all read from it — so content never drifts.
  - No-screenshot effects: since visuals come later, the "wow" has to come from
  interaction + type. Featured cards get cursor-tracked 3D tilt + a spotlight 
  that follows the pointer + an outlined name that fills on hover — monochrome,
  tactile, and it makes text-only cards feel premium.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Let me build the data layer first (AURA gets both the legacy LangChain control
  plane and the on-device MCP story):

❯ there is some bug , i tried clicking view all works and individial works i

● Good catch — and I see the bug. Routing is working (the URL is #/work and the
  page changed), but the scroll position didn't reset, so you're parked at the
  global footer at the bottom instead of the top of the All Work page.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Lenis fights window.scrollTo. With Lenis driving scroll, calling
  window.scrollTo(0,0) gets overridden on the next animation frame because Lenis
  still holds its old target scroll and hasn't recomputed the page height for
  the new (shorter) page. So the page swaps but you stay at the old pixel offset
  — which lands on the footer.
  - The fix: on route change, call lenis.resize() (recompute the new page's
  height) and lenis.scrollTo(0, { immediate, force }) inside a
  requestAnimationFrame (after the new DOM lays out). That makes the reset
  stick.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Fixing the route-change scroll reset:
